All sample sizes (n) represent independent biological replicates (mice): n = 4 per genotype in c; n = 3 per genotype in e. In f, sample sizes are as follows: male, n = 16 WT, n = 20 Angelman, n = 17 Het and n = 21 Rescue; female, n = 18 WT, n = 12 Angelman, n = 26 Het and n = 20 Rescue.

Of note, we found that Eif2ak1 is upregulated in Angelman mice and returned to baseline in Rescue mice (Extended Data Fig. 6d). Eif2ak1 encodes a kinase called HRI (haem-regulated inhibitor of translation), which is classically known to phosphorylate eIF2α in response to low levels of haem in the cell29. More recent work has found that HRI promotes mitophagy30, and that its expression is induced in neurons in response to proteasome inhibition31. Consistent with our RNA-seq results, HRI protein abundance was increased in the cortex of Angelman mice (Extended Data Fig. 6f,g), and in addition, we found that overexpression of ASNS in neurons was sufficient to increase HRI abundance (Extended Data Fig. 6j,k). By contrast, overexpression of ASNS did not activate GCN2 (general control nonderepressible 2), the kinase that phosphorylates eIF2α during the AAR (Extended Data Fig. 6h,i), nor did it induce changes in the two other upstream kinases PERK and PKR (Extended Data Fig. 6j,k). Together, these observations suggest that ASNS overabundance in Angelman mice drives a downregulation of mitochondrial and protein synthesis pathways, and that upregulation of the HRI kinase leads to increased phosphorylation of eIF2α.

We next examined protein synthesis directly, using two different strategies. First, we measured new protein synthesis in vivo by using intraperitoneal injections of l-azidohomoalanine (AHA). AHA is a cell- and blood–brain-barrier-permeable synthetic amino acid that is charged onto methionine tRNAs and incorporates into newly synthesized proteins32. Proteins containing AHA can be labelled and visualized using a process known as bioorthogonal noncanonical amino acid tagging (BONCAT)32. We extracted proteins from mouse brains 16 h after injection (Fig. 5a), and found that on average there was a four-to-five-fold reduction in the rates of new protein synthesis in Angelman mice, compared with WT mice (Fig. 5b,c). This deficit was greatly improved in our Rescue mice (65.949 ± 7.569 of WT). Second, we performed polysome profiling in all four genotypes by isolating actively translating ribosomes from the whole brain at P21 (Fig. 5d). Quantification of polysome/monosome ratios revealed a significant shift towards monosome fractions in Angelman mice, compared with WT mice (Angelman 72.94 ± 2.83% of WT; Het, 95.57 ± 4.32% of WT; Rescue, 95.08 ± 5.12% of WT), indicating decreased translational efficiency by polysomes (Fig. 5e). Again, this deficit was improved in our Rescue mice. We further examined whether ASNS overabundance alone is sufficient to slow new protein synthesis in neurons. Cultured neurons overexpressing ASNS were treated with AHA, and fluorescent noncanonical amino acid tagging (FUNCAT)33 was performed to visualize the incorporation of AHA. AHA fluorescence was reduced by around 50% in neurons overexpressing ASNS (Extended Data Fig. 7a,b), suggesting that ASNS overabundance causes a global slowing of protein synthesis.

Finally, we sought to understand how reduced protein synthesis contributes to disease-relevant phenotypes. Previous studies showed that microcephaly in Angelman model mice arises from impaired neuronal growth that stunts axon and dendrite development14,15. We hypothesized that a reduction in neuronal protein synthesis contributes to poor neuronal growth, underlying the microcephaly observed in these mice. We measured brain weights in all four genotypes at P21. Consistent with previous reports, we observed a significant reduction in brain weights in Angelman mice14. This deficit was greatly improved in Rescue mice (Fig. 5f), independent of sex (Extended Data Fig. 7c). A previous study reported that smaller neurons in Angelman model mice cause greater neuronal packing in the cortex, leading to increased neuronal densities14. We therefore analysed neuronal density, and found that this measure was also improved in our Rescue mice (Extended Data Fig. 7d,e). Altogether, these data suggest that ASNS overabundance triggers the ISR and reduces the rate of protein synthesis, which, in turn, negatively affects neuronal growth and development in AS.

Identifying disease-relevant substrates in AS remains difficult, given the promiscuity of UBE3A promiscuity and its modest effect on substrate abundance. Here, we addressed this challenge by using disease-linked UBE3A mutants4,5,34, combined with di-Gly purification and mass spectrometry. Although ASNS seems to be a major contributor to AS, not all phenotypes were rescued by ASNS correction, suggesting that other substrates contribute to overall pathology. Two limitations of our proteomics experiments are worth noting. First, although it is unlikely, we cannot rule out that endogenous UBE3A competed with overexpressed UBE3A for binding substrate proteins. Second, because our workflow relied on affinity purification of ubiquitinated proteins, low abundance or rapidly degraded proteins might be under-represented in our data. Future studies using deeper proteomic coverage or complementary approaches will be needed to fully define the UBE3A substrate landscape relevant to AS pathology.

Our study found that Asns expression is limited mainly to neurons (Fig. 2), matching the neuron-specific imprinting pattern of Ube3a. Asparagine is a non-essential amino acid that is readily attained through the diet, but at the blood–brain barrier, it must be transported using a bidirectional facilitated transporter system (System N)35. However, rather than driving the accumulation of asparagine into the brain, this system seems to remove it from the cerebrospinal fluid36. We hypothesize that neurons rely on ASNS to synthesize most, if not all, of the asparagine they need, and that perturbations to ASNS represent a point of susceptibility in development.

Homozygous loss of ASNS causes asparagine synthetase deficiency, a rare congenital condition characterized by microcephaly, global developmental delay, hypotonia, spastic quadriplegia and intractable seizures37. However, contrary to a loss of ASNS function, our study found increased levels of ASNS protein in Angelman model mice, suggesting that a gain in ASNS function causes aberrant phenotypes. In support of this idea, we found that overexpression of ASNS alone in neurons was sufficient to increase both p-eIF2α and HRI levels (Extended Data Fig. 6a,j), and that genetic reduction of ASNS expression rescued protein synthesis as well as many aberrant behavioural phenotypes (Figs. 3 and 5). GOF mutations in ASNS have yet to be established as a cause of neurodevelopmental disorders; however, our results suggest that such a mechanism is plausible.

Our finding that free amino acids in the brains of Angelman mice are elevated (Fig. 4b) is consistent with our observation that neuronal protein synthesis is reduced with the loss of UBE3A. However, contrary to our original hypothesis, we did not find an activation of the AAR in Angelman mice. The only ISR-associated gene that was upregulated in Angelman mice and rescued with Asns modulation was Eif2ak1, the gene that encodes HRI. It is notable that HRI is induced in neurons upon proteostatic stress31, and this observation, combined with UBE3A’s known role at the proteasome18,38,39,40, suggests that proteostatic stress has a crucial role in AS pathology. Moreover, HRI mediates both mitochondrial maintenance30 and protein synthesis31, two pathways that are highly implicated in the current study. Whether these changes result from increased asparagine production, or through an alternative unidentified activity of ASNS, remains to be elucidated.

Our study found a reduction in new protein synthesis and a reduction in polysome, but not monosome, fractions in neurons (Fig. 5d). Previous work has shown that monosomes are predominantly localized to neuronal processes and are capable of synthesizing proteins from at least a subset of transcripts41. Future studies will be required to address whether monosome-dependent forms of translation are preserved in Ube3am−/p+ neurons.

Finally, our results have therapeutic implications. Our finding that persistent stress signalling is central to AS pathology places it among a growing list of neurodevelopmental disorders in which disrupted proteostasis and heightened stress signalling are a common mechanism of pathology28,42,43. This suggests that targeting ASNS, HRI or the ISR could alleviate at least some symptoms in AS and could complement genetically targeted therapies to improve their safety and efficacy.

All animal procedures were approved by and performed in accordance with the guidelines of the Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee (IACUC) at Washington University in St. Louis. Mice were housed in a specific-pathogen-free (SPF) facility managed by the Division of Comparative Medicine at Washington University School of Medicine, which is fully accredited by AAALAC International. Mouse holding rooms were maintained in a controlled environment with an ambient temperature of 20–24 °C and a relative humidity of 40–60%. Mice were kept on a regulated 12-h light–12-h dark cycle and provided with standard laboratory chow and water ad libitum. Ube3a-deficient mice were generated previously44 and obtained through Jackson Laboratory (016590). The mice used throughout this study were all maintained on a C57BL6/J background. Angelman mice (Ube3am−/p+) were generated by crossing Ube3am+/p− heterozygous females with WT males. An Asns-heterozygous mouse was generated at the Washington University Mouse Genetics Core using a guide RNA to generate a deletion of a single nucleotide at the Q17 codon on exon 2 of Asns (chr. 6: 7689452, GRCm39 assembly). Mice were backcrossed at least seven generations with WT C57Bl/6J mice (Ube3am+/p+; Asns+/+) before use in the study. mRNA of Cas9 and the selected gRNA were injected into fertilized hybrid C57Bl/6J/JxCBA eggs at the Mouse Genetics Core at Washington University School of Medicine and validated by targeted Sanger sequencing. Mice were genotyped by incubating tail-tips overnight at 55 °C in tail digest buffer (0.1 M Tris pH 8.8, 0.5 M NaCl, 0.005 M EDTA and 0.5% SDS) containing 0.4 mg ml−1 proteinase K (Gold Biotechnology, P-480-SL10). Afterwards, proteinase K was inactivated by incubation at 95 °C for 10 min and genomic DNA was purified by isopropanol precipitation. PCR was performed by amplifying a 486-bp fragment using forward primer 5′-GGTGCAGGGTTTATGGTTGGA-3′ and reverse primer 5′-GCTTACCGCCTTGTGGTTGTAG-3′. The products were purified using a PCR purification kit (QIAGEN, 28106) and submitted for Sanger sequencing (GENEWIZ). PCR was performed using MyTaq DNA polymerase (Meridian Bioscience, BIO-25043) with an initial denaturation step of 95 °C for 1 min, followed by 30 cycles of denaturation at 95 °C for 15 s, annealing at 60 °C for 15 s and an extension step of 72 °C for 55 s.

HEK293T cells and Neuro2a cells, obtained from ATCC, were cultured in a 5% CO2 humidified incubator and tested regularly for mycoplasma contamination. Cells were maintained in Dulbecco’s modified Eagle’s medium (DMEM) pre-supplemented with GlutaMAX (Gibco, 10569044), 5% fetal bovine serum (FBS; Gibco, 16140071) and 1% antibiotic–antimycotic solution (Gibco, 15240096) containing 10,000 units ml−1 penicillin, 10,000 µg ml−1 streptomycin and 25 µg ml−1 of amphotericin B. For biochemical analysis, transfections were done using the FuGENE 6 transfection reagent (Promega, E2691), according to the manufacturer’s instructions.

We established a stable UBE3A-KD cell line (HEK293T) by using an shRNA (Sigma, TRCN0000012894; target sequence: CCCAATGATGTATGATCTAAA) against the UBE3A gene. A lentiviral construct containing a scrambled shRNA sequence (Sigma, SHC002) was used as a control. pLKO.1-puro-UBE3A shRNA and scrambled shRNA were transfected into HEK293T cells and stable clones were selected with 1 μg ml−1 puromycin. Despite consecutive optimization trials to maximize silencing efficiency, the viable stable clones consistently exhibited a moderate reduction of about 25% in steady-state UBE3A expression levels. However, this level of knockdown proved biologically sufficient to elicit robust biochemical alterations in downstream target proteins. For proteasome inhibition assays, cells were treated with 15 μM MG132 (Calbiochem, 474790) for 12 h before collection, and cells treated with an equal volume of dimethyl sulfoxide (DMSO) served as the vehicle control.

Primary cortical neurons were cultured and maintained using the B-27 Plus neuronal culture system (Gibco, A3653401) supplemented with N2 (Gibco, 17502048) and penicillin–streptomycin (Gibco, 15140122). In brief, embryos were isolated at embryonic day 14.5 (E14.5) and the cortex of WT and Angelman mice was dissected. The tissue was dissociated using a trypsin solution and mechanical trituration to obtain a single-cell suspension. After dissociation, cells were seeded onto cell culture dishes pre-coated with poly-d-lysine (Sigma-Aldrich, P0899). Half of the neuronal culture medium was replaced every 2 days until the desired day of the experiment.

MYC-tagged human UBE3A isoform 2 constructs used in this study, including WT, ligase dead (LD; C829A), UBE3A(T485A) and UBE3A(T485E), were cloned into the pCIG2 plasmid DNA as previously described4,5. This vector is driven by a chicken β-actin promoter with bicistronic GFP expression via an internal ribosome entry site (IRES). The WT HA-tagged ASNS construct, derived from human ASNS and containing the complete reading frame (accession: BC014621.2), was cloned into the pCIG2 plasmid using PCR to introduce SacI and XmaI sites. The constructed plasmids were verified by DNA sequencing (GENEWIZ) and purified using a HiSpeed Plasmid Midi Kit (QIAGEN, 12643). The oligonucleotide sequences used to generate WT HA–ASNS and five lysine mutants of ASNS (K83R, K168, K467, K478 and K504) are provided in Supplementary Table 6. The HA codons are shown in red, and the blue colour indicates the presence of restriction sites. Lysine mutations in the oligonucleotides used for mutagenesis are shown in bold. To create viral expression vectors, WT HA–ASNS was subcloned into pAAV-hSyn-EGFP plasmid DNA (Addgene, 50465) by PCR using a KpnI site. To separate ASNS from EGFP, we inserted a 2A self-cleaving peptide (T2A) at the 5′ end of ASNS. Control virus and AAV-hSyn-WT-HA-ASNS-T2A-EGFP were packaged into AAV2/9n capsids (Addgene, 112865) with an AAV helper plasmid and pAdDeltaF6 (Addgene, 112867), and transfected into HEK293T cells at a 1:1:1 molar ratio using PEI MAX (Polysciences, 24765-100). AAVs were purified using the AAVpro Purification Kit (Takara, 6666) and titred using a qPCR AAV titration kit (Applied Biological Materials, G931). Primary neurons were transduced with AAVs at a multiplicity of infection of 7.5.

HEK293T cells expressing UBE3A(T485A) and UBE3A(T485E) constructs were cultured in DMEM supplemented with high glucose, GlutaMAX supplement and pyruvate (Gibco, 10569044), 10% dialysed FBS (Gibco, 26400044), 1% antibiotic–antimycotic solution containing 10,000 units ml−1 penicillin, 10,000 µg ml−1 streptomycin and 25 µg ml−1 amphotericin B (Gibco, 15240096). For heavy labelling, 50 µg ml−1 13C6, 15N2 l-lysine (Lys8) and 40 µg ml−1 13C6, 15N4 l-arginine (Arg10) (Cambridge Isotope Laboratories, CNLM-291-H-PK and CNLM-539-H-PK, respectively) were added to the medium for cells expressing UBE3A(T485A). For cells expressing UBE3A(T485E) in light medium, standard 50 µg ml−1 l-lysine and 40 µg ml−1 l-arginine (Thermo Fisher Scientific, J62225.36 and A15738.36, respectively) were used. Cells were maintained in their respective labelled or standard medium for at least seven passages to ensure complete labelling. Subsequently, the cells were lysed using a lysis buffer (8 M urea in 50 mM Tris-HCl, 150 mM NaCl, 1 mM EDTA, 2 µg ml−1 aprotinin, 10 µg ml−1 leupeptin, 1 mM PMSF, 50 µM deubiquitinating enzyme (PR-619) and 1 mM iodoacetamide). The lysates were then centrifuged at 20,000g at 4 °C for 15 min, and the protein concentration was determined using a Pierce BCA protein assay kit (Invitrogen, 23227). Equal amounts of lysates from cells cultured in heavy and light media were mixed in a 1:1 ratio (mg:mg). The mixture was diluted in 50 mM Tris (pH 7.5) to a final concentration of 2 M urea. Total protein was enzymatically digested overnight at a 1:100 ratio (trypsin:protein) using sequencing-grade modified trypsin (Promega, V5111). The resulting peptides were purified on a Sep-Pak column (Waters) and eluted with a 40-ml step gradient of 10–50% acetonitrile and 0.1% trifluoroacetic acid (TFA) (10%, 15%, 20%, 23%, 30%, 35%, 40% and 50%: 5 ml each). Each fraction was lyophilized overnight. The lyophilized peptides were subjected to immunoaffinity purification using a PTMScan Ubiquitin Remnant Motif (K-ɛ-GG) Kit (Cell Signaling Technology, 5562). The peptide solution was transferred into the vial containing motif antibody beads, and the mixtures were incubated on a rotator for 4–6 h at 4 °C. K-ɛ-GG peptides were eluted with 50 μl of 0.15% TFA after washing the bead slurry with phosphate-buffered saline (PBS) and water. Eluted peptides were further purified using Pierce C18 spin columns (Thermo Fisher Scientific, 89870) following the manufacturer’s instructions. The peptides were eluted with 70% acetonitrile and 0.1% TFA solution in 50-μl volumes twice and then lyophilized using a SpeedVac.

For mass-spectrometry analysis, lyophilized peptides were resuspended in 1% acetonitrile and 0.1% formic acid. Each sample was analysed using LC–MS/MS (EASY nLC 1000 coupled with QExactive HF mass spectrometer; Thermo Fisher Scientific). The samples were injected onto an EASY-Spray PepMap C18 column (75 μm inner diameter × 25 cm; 2 μm particle size) and separated using a gradient consisting of 5–35% mobile phase B at a flow rate of 250 nl per min flow rate, with mobile phase A consisting of 0.1% formic acid in water and mobile phase B consisting of 0.1% formic acid in acetonitrile. The QExactive HF was operated in data-dependent mode, selecting the 15 most intense precursors for subsequent fragmentation. The resolution (m/z 400–1,600) for the precursor scan was set to 120,000, with a target value of 3 × 106 ions. The resolution of the MS/MS scan was set to 30,000, with a target value of 1 × 105 ions. For higher-energy collision dissociation, we set the normalized collision energy to 27% and the separation window to 1.6 m/z. We set peptide matches as preferred and excluded precursors with unknown charge or charge states of 1 and ≥7. Raw data files were processed using Proteome Discoverer (v.2.1) (Thermo Fisher Scientific). The peak list was searched against the reviewed human UniProt database (containing 20,203 entries) with the contaminant database added using Sequest (v.2.1). For protein identification, the following parameters were used to identify the trypsin peptide: 10 ppm precursor ion mass tolerance, 0.02-Da product ion mass tolerance, a maximum of two missing trypsin cleavage sites, carbamidomethylation of Cys set as a fixed modification and oxidation of Met, gly-gly of Lys, Ser/Thr/Tyr, [13C6]Arg, [13C6]Lys and acetylation of the N terminus set as variable modifications. We used Percolator (v.2.05) to estimate the number of false positive identifications and assigned a q-value of 0.05 (5% FDR), which was used to filter all results. We used the ptmRS node (v.2.1) to assign a probability of post-translational modification site localization. To determine the SILAC rate for ubiquitin-modified peptides, we enabled peptide quantification. For two replicates (experiments 2 and 3), we note that there was a polymer present in our di-Gly motif pull-downs that caused the LC column to become partially clogged, leading to a reduction in the number of total detected proteins. Consequently, owing to these replication-specific technical issues, the total numbers of detected proteins varied across individual replicates, and candidates were filtered on the basis of unique protein comparisons across all valid trials.

To assess how the identified candidate substrates are altered in human neurons, the compiled list of 55 previously proposed substrates, along with ASNS and DNA-PKc, was cross-referenced against a previously published embryonic-stem-cell-derived proteomic dataset from patient-derived AS neurons (Supplementary Table 2). This clinical dataset comprised upregulated protein profiles from two independent patient-derived backgrounds: a line containing a large maternal deletion encompassing UBE3A (ΔChr. 15q11.2–q12) and a line containing a premature truncating nonsense mutation in the maternal UBE3A allele (Q322X). Overlapping proteins between our candidate screen and the 532 commonly upregulated proteins in both patient lines were identified to extract high-confidence disease-relevant substrates.

Cultured cells or mouse brain tissue were lysed in ice-cold RIPA buffer for western blotting or cold modified RIPA buffer for immunoprecipitation. The composition of the RIPA buffer was as follows: 50 mM Tris-HCl (pH 7.4), 150 mM NaCl, 1% Triton X-100, 0.1% sodium dodecyl sulfate and 0.25% sodium deoxy-cholate. For western blotting, an EDTA-free protease (Thermo Fisher Scientific, 78437) and phosphatase (Thermo Fisher Scientific, 78428) inhibitor cocktail was added to the RIPA buffer. For immunoprecipitation, 5 mM EDTA and protease and phosphatase inhibitor cocktail were included in the modified RIPA buffer. After lysis, the samples were centrifuged, and protein concentrations were determined using a Pierce BCA protein assay kit (Invitrogen, 23227). Equal amounts of protein were resuspended in 1× LDS sample buffer (Thermo Fisher Scientific, 84788), separated by SDS–PAGE, and transferred onto nitrocellulose membranes (Cytiva, 10600002). Membranes were blocked in 1× fish gelatin blocking agent (Biotium, 22010) in Tris-buffered saline (TBS) containing 0.1% Tween 20 (TBST) for 1 h at room temperature. Afterwards, membranes were incubated with primary antibodies at 4 °C overnight. Bands were visualized using secondary antibodies, either IRDye 680RD donkey anti-mouse IgG (Li-Cor, 926-68072) or IRDye 800CW donkey anti-rabbit IgG (Li-Cor, 926-32213), used at a dilution of 1:10,000. Protein bands were analysed using the Odyssey CLx infrared imaging system and Image Studio v.5.2 software (Li-Cor Biosciences).

For immunoprecipitation analysis, lysates were pre-cleared using protein G Sepharose beads (Sigma-Aldrich, GE17-0618-01), for 1 h at 4 °C on an orbital shaker. An equal amount of pre-cleared lysate was incubated with the primary antibody for 12 h, followed by an additional 4-h incubation with protein G sepharose beads. The resulting immune complexes were isolated by centrifugation, washed at least three times with ice-cold RIPA buffer and resuspended in 2× SDS sample buffer. Protein complexes were resolved by SDS–PAGE and western blot analysis. The primary antibodies used were mouse anti-MYC (1:3,000, EMD Millipore, 05-724), rabbit anti-ASNS (1:3,000 or 1:7,500, Proteintech, 14681-1-AP), mouse anti-DNA-PKcs (1:1,000, Cell Signaling Technology, 12311), rabbit anti-RPL3 (1:3,000, Proteintech, 11005-1-AP), mouse anti-β-actin (1:5,000, Santa Cruz Biotechnology, sc-47778), mouse anti-GFP (1:5,000, Santa Cruz Biotechnology, sc-9996), rabbit anti-FASN (1:2,000, Proteintech, 10624-2-AP), rabbit anti-FANCI (1:3,000, Proteintech, 20789-1-AP), mouse anti-VCP (1:1,000, Invitrogen, MA3-004), rabbit anti-PYGL (1:1,000, Proteintech, 15851-1-AP), rabbit anti-TUBGCP2 (1:1,000, ABclonal, A10283 and 1:3,000, Proteintech, 25856-1-AP), mouse anti-KPNB1 (1:1,000, EMD Millipore, 05-1530), rabbit anti-WRNIP1 (1:1,000, ABclonal, A9170 and 1:2,000, Santa Cruz Biotechnology, sc-376438), rabbit anti-ALDOA (1:3,000, Proteintech, 11217-1-AP), rabbit anti-FBL (1:1,000, ABclonal, A1136), rabbit anti-EIF4A1 (1:2,000, ABclonal, A5294), mouse anti-Flag (1:3,000, Cell Signaling Technology, 8146), rabbit anti-Flag (1:3,000, Cell Signaling Technology, 14793), rabbit anti-E6AP (1:1,000, Bethyl, A300-351A), rabbit anti-LDHB (1:3,000, Proteintech, 14824-1-AP), rabbit anti-eIF2α (1:1,000, Cell Signaling Technology, 5324), rabbit anti-phospho-eIF2α (1:1,000, Cell Signaling Technology, 3398), mouse anti-HA (1:3,000, BioLegend, 901501), rabbit anti-HA (1:3,000, Abcam, ab9110), rabbit anti-ubiquitin (1:3,000, Cell Signaling Technology, 58395), mouse anti-ubiquitin (P4D1) (1:1,000, Santa Cruz, sc-8017), rabbit anti-GCN2 (1:1,000, Cell Signaling Technology, 3302), rabbit anti-GCN2 (phospho T899) (1:1,000, Abcam, ab75836), rabbit anti-PERK (1:1,000, Cell Signaling Technology, 3192), rabbit anti-phospho-PERK (1:1,000, Cell Signaling Technology, 3179), rabbit anti-EIF2AK2 (PKR) (1:1,000, Proteintech, 18244-1-AP) and rabbit anti-EIF2AK1 (HRI) (1:3,000, Proteintech, 20499-1-AP).

In vitro ubiquitination assays were done using recombinant human ubiquitin-activating enzyme UBE1 (R&D Systems, E-305), recombinant UBCH7 (UBE2L3; R&D Systems, E2-640), recombinant human E6AP (UBE3A; R&D Systems, E3-230), recombinant human ubiquitin (R&D Systems, U-100H), recombinant human ASNS (R&D Systems, 10193-AS-020), 10× E3 ligase reaction buffer (500 mM HEPEs (pH 8.0), 500 mM NaCl and 10 mM TCEP)) and ATP (Sigma, A9187). Assays were performed as described previously. In brief, a single reaction contained 0.1 µM UBE1, 1 µM UBE2L3 (UbcH7), 50 µM ubiquitin, 2 mM Mg2+-ATP, 0.25 µM UBE3A and 0.125 μM ASNS. The reaction was allowed to proceed at 37 °C for the indicated time. Reactions were stopped by adding SDS sample buffer and boiling for 5 min at 95 °C. The samples were subjected to western blotting using an anti-ASNS antibody (Proteintech, 14681-1-AP).

To detect in vivo ASNS ubiquitination, hippocampi were collected from P43 WT and Angelman mice and lysed under denaturing conditions using a lysis buffer containing 25 mM HEPES (pH 7.4), 1% Triton X-100, 15 mM KCl, 5 mM EDTA, 1% SDS and EDTA-free protease inhibitor cocktail (Thermo Fisher Scientific, 78437). The resulting lysates were boiled for 30 min, and cellular debris was removed by centrifugation at 15,000g. Equal amounts of lysate from each genotype were diluted 1:10 (v/v) in a lysis buffer without SDS. Lysates were pre-cleared with protein G sepharose beads and endogenous ASNS protein was immunoprecipitated using an ASNS antibody (Proteintech, 14681-1-AP) for 12 h at 4 °C. The final immune complexes were washed three times with a lysis buffer containing 125 mM NaCl and then boiled for 10 min at 95 °C in LDS sample buffer. The resulting samples were visualized using an anti-ubiquitin antibody (1:1,000, Cell Signaling Technology, 3936).

To monitor the ubiquitination of ASNS lysine mutants, HEK293T cells transfected with HA-tagged ASNS mutants and Flag-tagged ubiquitin were treated with 30 μM MG132 (Calbiochem, 474790) for 4 h to inhibit proteasome activity. Cells were then lysed in a lysis buffer containing 25 mM HEPES (pH7.4), 1% Triton X-100, 15 mM KCl, 5 mM EDTA, 1% SDS and EDTA-free protease inhibitor cocktail (Thermo Fisher Scientific, 78437). Lysates were diluted in lysis buffer without SDS and pre-cleared with protein G sepharose beads, and ASNS was immunopurified using an HA antibody (EMD Millipore, 05-904) for 4 h at 4 °C, followed by a further 12-h incubation with protein G Sepharose beads. Ubiquitination was detected using a Flag antibody in western blot analysis.

For brain tissue staining, WT and Angelman mice were anaesthetized and transcardially perfused with ice-cold PBS, followed by 4% paraformaldehyde (PFA), and the brains were dissected. Brains were further fixed overnight at 4 °C in 4% PFA. After fixation, the brains were cryopreserved and sectioned into 20–30-µm-thick slices. Sections were permeabilized with 0.5% Triton X-100 in PBS for 30 min, followed by blocking with a solution containing 5% bovine serum albumin (BSA), 1% normal donkey serum and 0.2% Triton X-100 in PBS for 1 h. Sections were incubated with primary antibodies (rabbit anti-ASNS, 1:800, Proteintech, 14681-1-AP; guinea pig anti-GABA, 1:500, EMD Millipore, AB175; guinea pig anti-NeuN; 1:3,000, EMD Millipore, ABN90; mouse anti-NeuN, 1:3.000, Abcam, ab104224; mouse anti-GFAP, 1:3,000, EMD Millipore, MAB360; goat anti-IBA1, 1:3,000, Novus Biologicals, NB100-1028; goat anti-5-HT, 1:3;000, ImmunoStar, 20079; mouse anti-TH, 1:3,000, ImmunoStar, 22941; sheep anti-neuropeptide Y, 1:1,000, EMD Millipore, AB1583; mouse anti-parvalbumin, 1:1,000, Swant, PV235; goat anti-calretinin, 1:1,000, Swant, CG1; rat anti-somatostatin, 1:1,000, EMD Millipore, MAB354; and mouse CaMKII-α, 1:1,500, Cell Signaling Technology, 50094) overnight for 4 °C. The following day, sections were incubated with secondary antibodies conjugated with Alexa Fluor dyes (488 AffiniPure donkey anti-rabbit, 1:2,000, Jackson ImmunoResearch Laboratories, 711-545-152; 647 AffiniPure donkey anti-guinea pig, 1:1,000, Jackson ImmunoResearch Laboratories, 706-605-148; 647 AffiniPure donkey anti-goat, 1:2,000, Jackson ImmunoResearch Laboratories, 705-605-147; 647 donkey anti-mouse, Thermo Fisher Scientific, A-31571; 647 AffiniPure donkey anti-rat, 1:1,000, Jackson ImmunoResearch Laboratories, 712-605-153; 568 donkey anti-mouse, 1:2,000, Invitrogen, A10037; and 568 donkey anti-sheep, 1:1,000, Thermo Fisher Scientific, A-21099) for 2 h at room temperature with gentle rocking. In the last 10 min of the incubation, nuclei were stained with Hoechst 33342 (Thermo Fisher Scientific, H3570) or DAPI (Sigma-Aldrich, D9542) for 10 min. Afterwards, sections were washed three times with PBST (PBS with 0.1% Tween 20), mounted on slides using Vectashield mounting medium (Vector Laboratories, H-1000) and imaged using an Olympus FV1299 confocal microscope. Fluorescence images were analysed using ImageJ software (v.1.53c).

To assess in vivo protein synthesis, we dissolved AHA (Thermo Fisher Scientific, C10102) in PBS. Mice were intraperitoneally injected at a dose of 50 μg AHA per g body weight. After 16 h, brains were collected (excluding the cerebellum) and a hemisphere was processed for BONCAT45. Proteins were extracted in RIPA buffer (20 mM Tris-HCl (pH 7.5), 150 mM NaCl, 1% Triton X-100, 0.1% sodium dodecyl sulfate, 1 mM EDTA, 1 mM EGTA and 1% sodium deoxy-cholate), supplemented with a protease (Thermo Fisher Scientific, 78437) and phosphatase (Thermo Fisher Scientific, 78428) inhibitor cocktail. To collect AHA-labelled proteins, 200 μg protein was incubated with 100 µM sDIBO-alkyne-conjugated biotin (Thermo Fisher Scientific, C20023) to biotinylate AHA-containing proteins through a copper-free click chemical reaction at room temperature for 2 h. Biotinylated proteins were then purified using streptavidin-conjugated dynabeads (50 μg; Thermo Fisher Scientific, 11205D). Purified proteins were detached from dynabeads by boiling in 1× SDS sample buffer. Eluted samples were processed for western blot analysis and visualized with the Revert 700 Total Protein Stain Kit (Li-Cor Biosciences, 926-11010).

Neurons cultured on coverslips were treated with 1 mM AHA (Click Chemistry Tools, 1066-100) for 4 h. Cells were then fixed in 4% PFA for 15 min at room temperature and processed for FUNCAT33. Fixed cells were blocked in a 5% BSA solution and incubated with 10 μM Click-iT Alexa Fluor 555 sDIBO alkyne (Invitrogen, C20021), a fluorescent compound that reacts with the azido group of proteins containing AHA, at room temperature for 2 h. Cell nuclei were stained with DAPI (Sigma-Aldrich, D9542). Coverslips were mounted in Vectashield (Vector Laboratories, H-1000) and imaged using an Olympus FV1200 scanning confocal microscope.

All snap-frozen hippocampal tissue from WT, Angelman, Het and Rescue mice were weighed at the start of the extraction. Metabolites were extracted from frozen hippocampus samples using an Omni Bead Ruptor Elite Homogenizer with an acetonitrile:methanol:water ratio of 2:2:1 at a volume of 40 μl per mg of tissue. Samples were incubated at −20 °C for 1 h, then centrifuged for 10 min at 20,000g at 4 °C. The supernatant was transferred into a LC–MS vial for analysis on the same day46. Ultra-high-performance LC (UHPLC)–MS was performed with a Thermo Fisher Scientific Vanquish Horizon UHPLC system interfaced with a Thermo Fisher Scientific Orbitrap ID-X Tribrid mass spectrometer. Polar metabolites were separated on a HILICON iHILIC-(P)-Classic column (100 × 2.1 mm, 5 μm). The mobile-phase solvents were composed of: A = 20 mM ammonium bicarbonate, 2.5 μM medronic acid and 0.1% ammonium hydroxide in water:acetonitrile 5:95. The column compartment was maintained at 45 °C. The following linear gradient was applied at a flow rate of 0.25 ml min−1: 0 min, 90% B; 12 min, 35% B; 12.5–14.5 min, 25% B; and 15 min, 90% B. The injection volume was 4 μl. Data were acquired in both positive- and negative-ion mode. LC–MS data were processed and analysed with the open-source Skyline software (v.23.1)47.

Total RNA was isolated using TRIzol reagent (Thermo Fisher Scientific, 15596026) according to the manufacturer’s instructions. cDNA synthesis was performed with a SMARTScribe reverse transcriptase (Takara, 639536). The following primers were used: forward, 5′-GCAGTGTCTGAGTGCGATGAA-3′ and reverse, 5′-TCTTATCGGCTGCATTCCAAAC-3′ for mouse Asns; forward, 5′-GGCTGTATTCCCCTCCATCG-3′ and reverse, 5′-CCAGTTGGTAACAATGCCATGT for mouse β-actin. For qPCR, we used the PowerUp SYBR Green master mix (Applied Biosystems, A25742) and reactions were performed on a QuantStudio 6 Flex Real-Time PCR machine (Applied Biosystems). Calculations of relative expression were performed using the comparative Ct method.

Before each test, mice were acclimatized to the testing room and the experimental set-up for several days. All tests were performed during the light period of the light–dark cycle, except for sleep and circadian rhythm experiments. We chose the number of mice used in our study according to published guidelines for Ube3am−/p+ mice12 and aimed to achieve a similar ratio of males and females in each genotypic group. Mice were tested by random distribution of genotypes and data analyses were performed by experimenters who were blinded to genotype.

In this study, a total of 138 mice were used for hindlimb clasping, marble burying and brain weight measurements (at P90 and P150): 33 WT (12 males, 21 females), 26 Angelman (15 males, 11 females), 40 Het (16 males, 24 females) and 39 Rescue (22 males, 17 females). For the open field and accelerating rotarod tests, a total of 71 mice were used: 16 WT (5 males, 11 females), 13 Angelman (5 males, 8 females), 19 Het (12 males, 7 females) and 23 Rescue (12 males, 11 females).

Mice at P35 were manually suspended by the tips of their tails at a distance of 30.5 cm from the surface of the bench. Each mouse underwent two trials with a 5-min interval between trials. The presence of hindlimb clasping behaviour was recorded for 30 s during each trial using a Sony HDR-CX405 camera. Hindlimb clasping behaviour was measured by the movement of the hindlimbs (one or both) retracting and curling inward toward the mouse’s body. Data are presented as the total time spent clasping across the two trials.

To assess general locomotor activity and approach or avoidance (anxiety-like) behaviours, P56 mice were placed individually in a 40 × 40-cm square chamber illuminated by a 30-lx LED light source. Locomotor activity was measured in the centre of the arena for 1 h, following published protocols48. The chamber was divided into specific zones: a central zone occupying the middle 50% of the total chamber area and a perimeter zone encompassing the outer 50%. Movement in the arena was quantified in terms of distance travelled, as well as time spent in and the number of entries into the centre and perimeter zones.

The accelerating rotarod test was performed at speeds ranging from 4 to 40 rpm over a duration of 300 s. Mice underwent a total of eight trials per day for four consecutive days, with all trials of the first day performed on a stationary rod for habituation. To assess their performance, the average time spent on the rotarod across all trials was calculated.

Polycarbonate cages with open tops were filled with a layer of aspen wood chips, approximately 4 cm deep, provided by the Division of Comparative Medicine at Washington University School of Medicine in St. Louis. On top of the aspen wood chips, a total of 20 black marbles were evenly arranged in a grid format of 5 rows and 4 columns. A mouse at P85 was allowed to interact with these marbles for a period of 45 min. Afterwards, the mouse was transferred back to its home cage and the cage with marbles was photographed. Marbles that were covered by aspen wood chips to an extent greater than 50% were counted as buried.

The Adapt-A-Base sleep monitoring system for mice (Signal Solutions, model MB-ACN10) is an automated and highly sensitive piezoelectric sleep monitor that uses short-duration (2-s) pressure-signal segments to ascribe a sleep or wake statistic. The non-invasive and high-throughput system detects breathing and gross body movements to analyse unsupervised sleep–wake patterns with 90% accuracy when compared with electroencephalogram (EEG) and electromyogram (EMG)-based methods49,50. Mice (n = 6 per group) were individually housed in Ancare N10 mouse cages (Ancare) and placed on the Adapt-A-Base systems in sound- and light-proof circadian cabinets (Actimetrics) under 12-h light–12-h dark cycle (light on from 06:00 to 18:00; 300 lx) with ad libitum access to food, water and nesting material. Each cage was furnished with 160 g corn-cob bedding, 220 g food pellets and 380 ml water so that each cage weighed the same except for a small variability between mouse body weight. After 5 days of acclimation, the sleep–wake states were recorded without disturbance over 7 days using PiezoSleep v.2.08 (Signal Solutions). Sleep data were analysed for multiple sleep features of individual mice using SleepStats v.2.18 (Signal Solutions), and 30-s epochs were used for scoring sleep or wake bout lengths.

Mice (n = 7–9 per group) were individually housed in Ancare N10 mouse cages (Ancare) mounted with passive infrared sensor wireless nodes (Actimetrics) and acclimated for 5 days. Daily locomotor activity was recorded at 1-min intervals using the ClockLab Wireless data collection program v.4.129 (Actimetrics) under 12-h light–12-h dark cycle (light on from 06:00 to 18:00; 300 lx) for the first 7 days, followed by constant dark for the next 21 days with ad libitum access to food, water and nesting material. Period length (tau) was determined by χ2 periodogram analysis, rhythm strength was measured by relative fast Fourier transform analysis and other features of circadian locomotor activity were measured using ClockLab Analysis v.6.1.02 (Actimetrics). All routine mouse care during the dark phase was performed under dim red light.

Cortices and hippocampi were dissected on ice in PBS from WT, Angelman, Het and Rescue littermates at P21 (n = 6 mice per genotype; WT, 3 males, 3 females; Angelman, 4 males, 2 females; Het, 3 males, 3 females; and Rescue, 3 males, 3 females). Tissue was flash-frozen in liquid nitrogen, lysed and the RNA was extracted using the RNeasy Plus Mini Kit (QIAGEN, 74134) following the manufacturer’s instructions. Libraries were generated from 10 ng of RNA using the SMART-Seq Stranded Kit (Takara, 634443) for Illumina and sequenced on an Illumina NextSeq 550 1×75 bp at the Edison Family Center for Genomic Sciences and Systems Biology at Washington University School of Medicine. Our sequencing yielded an average of 8.20 × 106 75-bp single-end reads per sample. RNA-seq quality control was performed using FastQC51 (v.0.11.9) and MultiQC52 (v.1.9), followed by adapter and quality trimming with Trim Galore (v.0.6.7; Q ≥ 20, minimum length 36 bp). Ribosomal RNA reads were removed by Bowtie253 (v.2.4.4) alignment to mouse rRNA sequences (NR_003278.3, NR_003279.1 and NR_046144.1), and the remaining reads were aligned to the GRCm39 genome (GCF_000001635.27) using STAR54 (v.2.7.11b), allowing up to ten multi-mapping locations per read. Gene-level counts were generated with featureCounts55 (v.2.1.1; Subread) using reverse strand specificity (-s 2), counting exon-mapped reads and excluding multi-mapping reads. Differential expression analysis was performed using edgeR56 (v.4.0.16) in R after retaining genes with counts per million (CPM) > 1 in fewer than three samples and TMM normalization; generalized linear models included sex (~Sex + Genotype) for tissue-specific analyses with significance assessed by quasi-likelihood F-tests (FDR < 0.05). For exon-level analysis of Ube3a (NM_011668.3) and Asns (NM_012055.5), featureCounts was run in exon mode (-f) with reverse strand specificity (-s 2) and fractional assignment of multi-mapping reads (-M–fraction), and exon counts from RefSeq Select transcripts were normalized by exon length to obtain counts per kilobase.

Using the Allen Institute’s whole-mouse-brain single-cell RNA-seq dataset, we calculated the mean and variance of Asns log2 expression and its corresponding log2 expression z-score (computed using the scanpy.tl.score_genes function57) for each annotated cell type identified58. These gene-expression scores were then mapped onto the corresponding cell types in the Allen Institute’s annotated MERFISH spatial transcriptomics dataset58. Spatial maps were generated by colouring each cell type according to its Asns expression using the ggplot2 package59 in R (v3.4.4).

The single-cell genomics tool Seurat (v.4)60 was used for scRNA-seq data analysis. We followed the workflow described in the Seurat guided analysis on datasets normalized with SCTransform to normalize gene-expression levels. Principal component analysis was performed using the top 30 principal components. Uniform manifold approximation and projection (UMAP) analysis was performed with the top 30 dimensions. Clusters were identified with the functions FindNeighbors (dims = 1:30) and FindClusters (resolution = 0.8). A resolution of 0.8 was selected for the downstream analysis because clusters were clearly separated and matched visual inspection. Default parameters were used unless noted. For identified clusters, cluster marker analysis confirmed that they were transcriptionally distinct, with their unique marker genes. Major cell types and five different neuronal cell populations were annotated on the basis of the expression of unique markers as described in the original publication23, including astrocytes (Gfap, Aldh1l1 and Aqp4), endothelial cells (Ly6c1), oligodendrocytes (Mobp, Mog and Plp1), perivascular macrophages (Mrc1 and Lyve1), mural cells (Acta2), microglia (Tmem119, P2ry12 and Cx3cr1), ependymoma cells (Foxj1) and neuronal subpopulations N-Tbr1, N-Grp, N-Lhx6, N-Synpr and N-Spink8.

Statistical analyses were performed using GraphPad Prism (v.10).

The RNA-seq datasets generated in this study have been deposited in the Gene Expression Omnibus (GEO) database under accession code GSE319851. Proteomic, transcriptomic and metabolomic datasets are provided in Supplementary Tables 1, 3 and 4. The lists of genes identified from all group comparisons in the RNA-seq analysis are provided in Supplementary Table 5 (sheets 4 and 5). Previously published human induced pluripotent stem cell TMT mass-spectrometry datasets that were reanalysed in this study were retrieved from the MassIVE repository under the accession number MSV000087654 (ref. 6). The published mouse whole-brain and hippocampus transcriptomic datasets from the Allen Institute22 were retrieved from the GEO database under accession code GSE185862, and are also available through the Allen Brain Map data portal. The published mouse cortex and hippocampus scRNA-seq dataset23 was retrieved from the GEO database under accession code GSE60361. Source data are provided with this paper.

The custom scripts used for RNA-seq data analysis are provided in Supplementary Table 5 (sheet: scripts). To ensure long-term preservation and compliance with reproducibility standards, the identical computational pipelines—including the workflows for GRCm39 genome alignment filtering, exon-level feature quantification and sex-adjusted differential expression analysis using edgeR—have also been deposited in Zenodo with open access at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22259707 (ref. 61).

a,b, Western blot (a) and quantification (b) of 11 putative UBE3A substrates from HEK293T cells expressing MYC-tagged WT UBE3A or UBE3A(LD). c,d, Western blot (c) and quantification (d) of 14 putative UBE3A substrates from HEK293T cells expressing MYC-tagged WT UBE3A and the GOF mutant UBE3A(T485A). e,f, Western blot (e) and quantification (f) of 15 putative UBE3A substrates from stable knockdown UBE3A (UBE3A KD) or scrambled control (Scr). g,h, Western blot (g) and quantification (h) of DNA-PKc, ASNS, and RPL3 from HEK293T cells expressing MYC-tagged WT UBE3A, UBE3A(LD), or WT UBE3A treated with MG132. The western bands shown were not all derived from the same western blot. For quantitative panels, data represent mean percentage ± s.e.m. of protein levels relative to controls. Statistical significance was determined using a two-sided one-way ANOVA with Tukey’s multiple-comparison test for b,d,h, and a two-tailed unpaired t-test for f; exact P values are indicated within the figure panels. All sample sizes (n) represent independent biological replicates: for b, n = 4 for FASN, VCP, LDHB, EIF4A1, and n = 3 for all other proteins; for d, n = 6 for DNA-PKc, ASNS, RPL3, and n = 4 for all other proteins; for f, n = 8 for DNA-PKc, ASNS, RPL3, and n = 4 for all other proteins; for h, n = 3 for all groups.

Arrow shows unmodified ASNS; arrowhead indicates a non-specific band. Merged images include DAPI (blue). Arrowheads/arrows indicate presence/absence of ASNS colocalization. Scale bar, 120 µm. Colocalization analysis (g) on neurons expressing ASNS and parvalbumin (PV), somatostatin (SST), calretinin (CR), and neuropeptide Y (NPY). Cells with >50% marker signal threshold within ASNS-positive neurons were analysed. h–k, Confocal images (h–j) and quantification (k) of ASNS in NPY-positive interneurons and CaMKIIα-positive excitatory neurons in the hippocampus of WT and Angelman mice. Scale bars, 100 μm. For quantitative panels, data represent mean percentage ± s.e.m. of levels relative to controls, except for d (mean intensity ± s.e.m. in arbitrary units). Statistical significance was determined using a two-tailed unpaired t-test for b,d, and a two-sided one-way ANOVA with Tukey’s multiple-comparison test for g,k. Exact P values are indicated within the figure panels. All sample sizes (n) represent independent biological replicates; n = 4 for b, n = 47 individual cells analysed from n = 5 independent experiments per group for d, n = 3 mice for NPY, SST, CR and n = 6 mice for PV for e–g, n = 4 mice per group for h–k.

Solid symbols represent females, while open symbols indicate males. f,g, Total sleep (f) and bout length (g) in P68 mice. h, Circadian activity in P90 mice. For quantitative panels, data represent mean ± s.e.m. Statistical significance was determined using a two-tailed unpaired t-test for b,c, a two-sided one-way ANOVA with Tukey’s multiple-comparison test for e, and a two-sided two-way ANOVA with Tukey’s multiple-comparison test for d,f–h. Exact P values are indicated within the figure panels. All sample sizes (n) represent independent biological replicates (individual mice): n = 4 per genotype for b,c; for d, P0 (n = 3 WT, 4 Angelman, 13 Het, 10 Rescue), P10 (n = 12 WT, 4 Angelman, 19 Het, 17 Rescue), P21 (n = 16 WT, 22 Angelman, 18 Het, 22 Rescue), and P90/P150 (n = 12 WT, 15 Angelman, 15 Het, 22 Rescue); for e, n = 71 total mice (WT: 5 males, 11 females; Angelman: 7 males, 8 females; Het: 12 males, 7 females; Rescue: 12 males, 11 females); n = 6 mice per genotype for f,g; and for h, n = 8 WT, 7 Angelman, 7 Het, and 9 Rescue mice.

Box plots show the 25th–75th percentiles (bounds of box) with a median centre line and minimum-to-maximum range whiskers. d,e, Confocal projections (d) of the primary somatosensory area with NeuN and Hoechst 33342, and density quantification (e). Exact P values are indicated within the figure panels. All sample sizes (n) represent independent biological replicates (individual cells for b; individual mice for c–e): n = 55 cells from 10 independent experiments for EGFP and n = 65 cells from 15 independent experiments for ASNS-EGFP for b, males (n = 16 WT, 22 Angelman, 18 Het, 22 Rescue) and females (n = 18 WT, 13 Angelman, 26 Het, 22 Rescue) for c, and n = 4 mice per group for d,e.

#3376. Dancing Magnet Demonstration

Magnets are always fun when used in science experiments and demonstrations. They are fairly inexpensive to buy and can be used in lots of creative ways. We still play with our magnet cars years after making them!

This simple dancing magnet activity uses a basic frame to hold a magnet in place while other magnets make it dance.

Create a pyramid frame with the straws and magnets.

Tie a piece of string to one magnet and hang it from the centre of the frame.

Place another magnet under the hanging magnet and watch it move.

Place magnets in different places around the frame and watch your swinging magnet dance.

Take a look at my other magnet experiments to learn more about magnetism.

Magnetism is an invisible field. We can’t see it, but we can feel it when magnets are placed close to each other.

A magnetic field is the area where other magnets or magnetic materials experience a force.

Magnets have two ends called poles. A north pole and a south pole.

If you put two poles that are the same together, you can feel them pushing each other apart ( they repel each other ). Two different poles attract each other.

The lines on a magnetic field diagram show which way a force would act on a north pole at that point in the field. The closer together the lines are, the stronger the magnetic field.

These are made from a magnetised material and have a permanent magnetic field.

Magnets can also be created using electricity. This type of magnet is called an electromagnet. The magnetic field only exists when an electric current is flowing. Electromagnets are helpful as the magnetic effect can be turned on and off.

These are very strong magnets made from rare earth elements such as neodymium and samarium.

Some animals can sense magnetic fields. This is called magnetoreception.

The further away from a magnet you get, the weaker the field is.

Magnets were used in compasses over 2000 years ago in China! You can make a compass with a magnet, a steel needle and a bowl of water.

The Earth’s magnetic field is generated by its molten iron core.

When not near a magnet, compasses always point to the Earth’s North Pole.

Magnets can be used to make objects appear to float! Maglev ( magnetic levitation ) trains use magnets to float above the tracks. This gives a smoother, quieter ride and the ability to travel faster than traditional trains.

Magnets can be demagnetised by hammering. The force of the hammering makes molecules inside magnets lose their north-south alignment.

Magnets can also be demagnetised by heating.

Last Updated on September 18, 2025 by Emma Vanstone

#3377. You Yeong-Gwang: Der Weg eines südkoreanischen Bestsellerautors

Sein Roman "Der Regenzaubermarkt" erzählt die Geschichte eines geheimnisvollen, verborgenen Ladens, der nur dann öffnet, wenn es regnet. Und Glücksgefühle. Das Buch wurde zu einem außergewöhnlichen Erfolg.

Zehn Jahre lang hatten Verlage die Manuskripte von You Yeong-Gwang abgelehnt. Auch Crowdfunding, um eine Veröffentlichung selbst zu finanzieren, führte nicht zum Ziel.

Dann startete der Autor einen letzten Versuch, bevor er aufgeben wollte. Mit überraschendem Erfolg: Ein Verlag sagte ja. Und noch bevor das Buch in Südkorea gedruckt wurde, bemühten sich internationale Verlage darum, die Übersetzungsrechte zu erwerben.

"Ich bin unglaublich dankbar, dass das Buch inzwischen in mehr als 20 Ländern veröffentlicht wurde", freute sich You im Gespräch mit der DW auf der Frankfurter Buchmesse 2026. "Und noch erstaunlicher ist, dass es sogar auf der Bestsellerliste des Spiegels in Deutschland landete."

Für einen Debütroman eines unbekannten Autors war dies ein gewaltiger Erfolg: Übersetzungen in 15 Sprachen, darunter Englisch, Deutsch, Spanisch und traditionelles Chinesisch.

Der Regenzaubermarkt erzählt die Geschichte eines geheimnisvollen Ladens, der nur bei Regen öffnet. Besucher können dort ihr tiefstes Unglück gegen ein Ticket für ein völlig neues Schicksal eintauschen.

Im Mittelpunkt der Geschichte steht die niedergeschlagene junge Serin, die auf den von fantasievollen Geistern betriebenen Markt stößt und dort einen warmen, tröstlichen Zufluchtsort findet.

"Serins Geschichte ist eigentlich meine Geschichte", sagt You. "Lange Zeit habe ich mich gefragt, wie wunderbar es wäre, mein Unglück zu verkaufen und mit dem Geld Glück kaufen zu können."

Während des Schreibens machte der Autor selbst eine schwierige Lebensphase durch. "Ich musste mich um meine Mutter kümmern, die lange krank war. Außerdem war ich viele Jahre lang finanziell für meine Familie verantwortlich. Das Leben fühlte sich unglaublich schwer an", erinnerte er sich.

"Doch beim Schreiben konnte ich vollkommen in die Welt der Geschichte eintauchen und zumindest für kurze Zeit meine Situation vergessen. Deshalb machte mich das Schreiben so glücklich. Ich habe es wirklich genossen."

Die Idee des Regens leitete sich direkt aus seinem eigenen Leben ab. "Damals fühlten sich meine Lebensumstände so an, als würde es ununterbrochen regnen", sagt You.

Und die Geschichte erinnert dabei stark an traditionelle koreanische Erzählungen. Sie gilt als ein typisches Beispiel für sogenannte "Literatur der Heilung" (Healing Fiction): Geschichten, in denen die junge Hauptfigur erkennen muss, welchen Preis ein scheinbar perfektes Leben tatsächlich hat.

"Während des Schreibens habe ich nicht bewusst versucht, Healing Fiction zu verfassen", stellt der Autor jedoch klar. "Damals wusste ich nicht einmal wirklich, dass es diesen Begriff überhaupt gibt."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Ursprünglich wollte You das Buch selbst veröffentlichen und startete dafür eine Crowdfunding-Kampagne. Doch als diese scheiterte, investierte er seine gesamten Ersparnisse und ließ 200 Exemplare drucken.

Buchhandlungen wollten das Werk jedoch nicht ins Sortiment aufnehmen. In einem Monat verkaufte er lediglich fünf Exemplare. "Ich dachte: Das war's. Jetzt muss ich wirklich aufgeben."

Zu dieser Zeit musste er sich zudem einer Augenoperation unterziehen. Aufgrund von Komplikationen konnte er zeitweise nicht sehen und begann deshalb, in einem Callcenter zu arbeiten.

"Unter der Woche arbeitete ich im Callcenter, und an Wochenenden besuchte ich eine Computerschule. Mehrere Jahre lang lernte ich, wie man Bücher selbst schreibt."

Während einer weiteren Crowdfunding-Kampagne arbeitete er außerdem als Essenslieferant. "Die Hin- und Rückfahrt zwischen meinem Zuhause und meiner Servicezone dauerte jeden Tag etwa vier Stunden. Deshalb schrieb ich während der U-Bahn-Fahrten in der Notiz-App meines Telefons."

Schriftsteller sein war für ihn Erholung: "Selbst in sehr schwierigen Zeiten konnte ich weiterschreiben, weil die Momente des Schreibens so viel Freude bereiteten. Für kurze Zeit konnte ich die schwierige Situation vergessen, in der ich mich befand."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"Ich sammelte all diese Texte und startete das, was eigentlich meine letzte Crowdfunding-Kampagne werden sollte", sagte er. Die Kampagne sollte die Veröffentlichung von Der Regenzaubermarkt finanzieren.

"Zu meinem Erstaunen war die Kampagne erfolgreich, und ich konnte das Buch zum ersten Mal kommerziell veröffentlichen." Nach dem Erfolg der Finanzierung wurde der Roman von Clayhouse, einem bedeutenden südkoreanischen Verlag, übernommen.

Und noch vor der Veröffentlichung erhielt You Angebote von Verlagen aus fünf Ländern und unterzeichnete internationale Verlagsverträge. Der Berliner Ueberreuter Verlag brachte die deutsche Ausgabe heraus.

Bereits im ersten Monat nach Erscheinen wurden über alle Formate hinweg 30.000 Exemplare verkauft. Das Buch war sowohl das meistverkaufte E-Book als auch das drittmeistverkaufte gedruckte Buch.

"Es fällt mir immer noch schwer zu glauben, dass all das passiert ist und ich nun auf der Frankfurter Buchmesse stehe. Ich bin unglaublich dankbar und glücklich", sagt You.

Südkoreas neueste Form kultureller Soft Power ist die Literatur. Im Jahr 2025 wurden zum zweiten Mal in Folge rund 1,2 Millionen übersetzte koreanische Bücher im Ausland verkauft.

Yous Roman gehört dabei zu einem derzeit besonders erfolgreichen Genre: der sogenannten Korean Comfort Fiction. Das neue Genre erfüllt das menschliche Bedürfnis nach Eskapismus und bietet eine vorübergehende Zuflucht vor einem hochkompetitiven Bildungssystem, einem harten Arbeitsmarkt und enormem Leistungsdruck im Berufsleben.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"Wenn die Leser das Buch schließen, möchte ich, dass sie etwas Wärme im Herzen spüren", sagt You. "Als ich schrieb, befand ich mich selbst in einer sehr schwierigen Phase und wünschte mir, jemand würde mich trösten. Also dachte ich: Warum lese ich nicht etwas in dieser Art? Warum schreibe ich nicht etwas, das mich selbst trösten kann?"

Immer mehr Leser weltweit suchen nach Geschichten, die die Seele beruhigen und Figuren zeigen, mit denen sie sich identifizieren können. Anders als in klassischen Heldengeschichten müssen die Protagonisten oft keinen Bösewicht besiegen.

Stattdessen sehnen sie sich nach Ruhe, Gemeinschaft und emotionaler Heilung. Der fantasievolle Aspekt dieser Romane ermöglicht ihnen zugleich, für einen Moment der Realität zu entfliehen.

Das Genre gewann zunächst durch unabhängige Veröffentlichungen, Crowdfunding-Kampagnen und Empfehlungen prominenter K-Pop-Stars an Popularität. Auch international wurde der Erzählstil schnell erfolgreich.

Kritiker bemängeln, dass die Romane oft einem vorhersehbaren Muster folgen, sich um einen einzelnen magischen Ort drehen und eher auf Wohlfühlatmosphäre als auf tiefgehende Charakterentwicklung setzen.

Der Literaturkritiker Peter Juhl bezeichnete den Regenzaubermarkt in einer Rezension für die Korea Times als leichte Wohlfühl-Lektüre und argumentierte, der Boom des Genres spiegle den gezielten Versuch wider, international vermarktbare Romane für den Weltmarkt zu schreiben.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"Natürlich freue ich mich darüber, dass mein Buch so viel Zuspruch erhält und ich schreiben kann. Doch mehr als alles andere hoffe ich, dass meine Mutter ihre Gesundheit zurückerlangt", sagt der Bestsellerautor. Er erwähnte, dass seine Mutter eine Woche vor seiner Ankunft auf der Buchmesse operiert worden war.

Über das Buch hinaus scheinen viele Menschen daher auch von seiner persönlichen Lebensgeschichte berührt zu sein. "Wenn ich die Möglichkeit habe, möchte ich noch mehr Geschichten über Hoffnung und Träume mit Menschen teilen, die sich in ähnlichen Situationen befinden wie ich", sagt You. "Wenn meine Geschichte auch nur einem einzigen Menschen ein wenig helfen kann, dann könnte mich nichts glücklicher machen."

Aus dem Englischen adaptiert von Florian Weigand.

#3379. Stichwahl in Brasilien: Lula triumphiert. Doch der Bolsonaro-Clan gibt nicht auf

Brasilien drohte mit Bolsonaro ein Präsident, der die Demokratie zertrümmern wollte. Aber Lula gelang die Wende. Sein Sieg zeigt, wie sich Rechtsextreme schlagen lassen.

#3380. Lula gewinnt Wahl - Bolsonaro erkennt Niederlage nicht an

Der linke Staatschef Lula hat die Präsidentschaftswahl in Brasilien gewonnen. Er setzte sich gegen den rechten Kandidaten Bolsonaro durch - doch der will das Ergebnis nicht anerkennen.

Der brasilianische Präsident Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva ist nach Angaben der Wahlbehörde für eine vierte Amtszeit gewählt worden. Er gewann demnach in Stichwahl gegen Flávio Bolsonaro, den Sohn des unter Hausarrest stehenden rechtsextremen Ex-Präsidenten Jair Bolsonaro.

Lula kam demnach auf 51,8 Prozent und Bolsonaro auf 48,2. Damit holte der Amtsinhaber fast 4,4 Millionen mehr Stimmen als Bolsonaro, wie das Oberste Wahlgericht mitteilte. Meinungsforschern zufolge verhalf eine unerwartet hohe Wahlbeteiligung Lula zum Sieg.

Bolsonaro weigerte sich, seine Niederlage gegen Lula einzugestehen. "Es ist unmöglich, das Ergebnis anzuerkennen", sagte Bolsonaro in Brasilia. "Ich werde die Annullierung dieser Wahlen beantragen", fügte Bolsonaro hinzu.

Bolsonaro begründete seine Forderung mit der kurzfristigen Entscheidung des Obersten Gerichtshofs, die Wahllokale zwei Stunden länger geöffnet zu lassen. Die Entscheidung sei beispiellos gewesen, erklärte er.

Allerdings hatten beide Kandidaten beantragt, Wahllokale länger geöffnet zu halten, Bolsonaro aber nur in bestimmten Bundesstaaten. Hintergrund waren Probleme im öffentlichen Nahverkehr sowie schlechtes Wetter in Teilen des Landes.

Lula, der diese Woche 81 Jahre alt wird, bezeichnete seinen Wahlsieg auch als Abstimmung über die Demokratie. Die Brasilianer hätten dafür gestimmt, "die Demokratie zu sichern", sagte Lula. Auf den Straßen feierten Tausende Anhänger seinen Sieg.

Die Wahl in Lateinamerikas größter Volkswirtschaft galt als Richtungsentscheidung. Lula und Bolsonaro vertreten in zahlreichen Politikfeldern gegensätzliche Positionen, etwa bei den Beziehungen zu den USA, beim Klimaschutz und in der Sozialpolitik.

Bolsonaro war von seinem Vater ins Rennen geschickt worden. Jair Bolsonaro ist wegen eines Umsturzversuchs zu mehr als 27 Jahren Haft verurteilt worden. Aus gesundheitlichen Gründen steht der frühere Präsident derzeit unter Hausarrest.

Lula oder Bolsonaro - beide Präsidentschaftskandidaten liegen vor der Stichwahl in Brasilien gleichauf. mehr

Umfragen hatten ein Kopf-an-Kopf-Rennen zwischen Lula und Bolsonaro bei der Stichwahl vorausgesagt. Die erste Wahlrunde Anfang Oktober hatte Bolsonaro überraschend mit 47 Prozent der Stimmen gewonnen. Lula kam damals auf 45 Prozent.

Bolsonaros Familienname bleibt sein wichtigstes politisches Kapital - und gleichzeitig seine größte Schwäche. mehr

Mit dem Sieg des Gegners von US-Präsident Donald Trump wurde ein Rechtsruck in Lateinamerika gestoppt. In diesem Jahr hatten Trump-Verbündete in Chile, Peru und Kolumbien Wahlsiege errungen.

Der linke Präsident konnte mit wirtschaftlichen Erfolgen punkten. Unter seiner Regierung sank die Arbeitslosigkeit auf einen historischen Tiefstand, die Armut ging zurück und der Mindestlohn stieg. Zudem beschloss das Parlament eine schrittweise Einführung der Fünf-Tage-Woche mit zwei freien Tagen. Gleichzeitig blieb der Unmut im Volk über die gestiegenen Lebenshaltungskosten groß.

Dieses Thema im Programm: tagesschau24 | Nachrichten | 26.10.2026 | 01:35 UhrNDRInfo | Nachrichten | 26.10.2026 | 03:30 Uhr

#3381. Yemen'de Husilerin fırlattığı balistik füzenin düşmesi sonucu Aden kentinde şiddetli patlama meydana geldi

Yerel kaynaklardan edinilen bilgiye göre, Husilerin attığı balistik füze Aden'in batısındaki Bureyka ilçesinde bulunan demir fabrikasının arkasındaki Bir Ahmed bölgesine düştü.

Hükümet güçlerine ait askeri kampın yakınındaki boş araziye düşen füze nedeniyle şiddetli patlama sesi duyuldu, olayda yaralanan olmadı.

Yemen İçişleri Bakanlığı, Husilerin ülkenin geçici başkenti Aden'deki Sahil Güvenlik birimlerine 3 balistik füzeyle saldırı düzenlediğini bildirmiş, Husiler de kentteki askeri yığınakları hedef aldıklarını ileri sürmüştü.

Yemen, İran destekli Husilerin Eylül 2014'te başkent Sana'yı ve bazı vilayetleri kontrol etmesinin ardından başlayan iç savaşa sahne oluyor.

Suudi Arabistan liderliğindeki Arap Koalisyonu, Mart 2015'ten bu yana uluslararası toplum tarafından tanınan Yemen hükümetine destek veriyor.

Yemen'de hükümet güçleri ile Husiler arasındaki çatışmalar son dönemde artarken İran destekli Husiler, kendilerine yönelik saldırılardan sorumlu tuttukları Suudi Arabistan'ı balistik füze ve insansız hava araçlarıyla hedef alıyor.

Anadolu Ajansı web sitesinde, AA Haber Akış Sistemi (HAS) üzerinden abonelere sunulan haberler, özetlenerek yayımlanmaktadır.Abonelik için lütfen iletişime geçiniz.

Bu haberde bir hata mı var? Her türlü talep, şikâyet ve görüş için okur temsilcimize ulaşabilirsiniz.

#3384. The Loss of Glaciers Is Inflicting a Spiritual Toll on Indigenous People

Indigenous people in the Peruvian Andes undertake a pilgrimage to the Qulqipunku glacier. The glacier is retreating as the climate warms. Salwan Georges / The Washington Post via Getty Images

In mountain regions from the Andes to the Himalayas, Indigenous people see the retreat of glaciers as a sign that they have lost the favor of their gods or ancestors.

“In many regions, mountains, glaciers and the water originating from them maintain central roles in local religious beliefs and practices,” researchers write in a recent paper. “As such, changing mountain landscapes and disappearing glaciers are not just physical consequences of climate change but also endanger the deep connection of people with their environment.”

Since 2000, glaciers around the world have lost 5 percent of their ice. While this trend is driven by warming, in many alpine regions people consider the retreat of glaciers a reflection of their own actions, according to the paper, published in Nature Climate Change.

Indigenous people return from their pilgrimage to the Qulqipunku glacier. Salwan Georges / The Washington Post via Getty Images

In the Peruvian Andes, the Quechua people make a yearly pilgrimage to the Qulqipunku glacier. Historically, pilgrims would gather ice from the glacier. Now, as the glacier retreats, they are opting to collect meltwater instead. The decline of the glacier, many believe, is a sign that the mountain spirit is tired of listening to their prayers, according to the paper.

The Aymara people of the Bolivian Andes see the glaciers around the Milluni Valley as their ancestors. When, in 2009, the Chacaltaya glacier melted away, the Aymara believed they were losing their protectors, researchers say. As the ice recedes, many see their retreat as a sign of spiritual imbalance, researchers say. In the Himalayas, traditional people also believe the peaks and glaciers are the homes of protective deities. Similar dynamics are at play in the Rwenzori Mountains of East Africa.

“Local communities express feelings of loss and culpability as their home landscapes are irrevocably altered,” said lead author Elizabeth Allison, of the California Institute of Integral Studies in San Francisco. “Traditional prophecies depict ultimate ruin in the event of the glaciers’ disappearance.”

The Race to Save Glacial Ice Records Before They Melt Away

#3385. Easy two ingredient science experiments for kids

One of my aims with Science Sparks is to make science as accessible as possible to kids and parents of all ages. I try to keep all the experiments as simple as I can, but the list of materials can often mount up and be a little overwhelming. With that in mind, I’ve compiled a list of my favourite two ingredient science experiments to make science at home easier than ever!

Some of the activities need tape or scissors, which I haven’t included in the two ingredient limit.

Make a cone shape from the paper, pop the cone on top of the milk carton and squeeze hard. The paper cone will shoot up into the air!

Extras – a container or bath and scissors

All you need to make magic opening flowers is paper and water! Cut out flower shapes ( or use my handy template ), fold up the petals and place the folded flower in water.

The paper absorbs the water, opening up the flower!

This is a great science activity for learning about capillary action and transport of water.

Learn a little science magic by making a lemon sink! This simple science activity is a fun way to learn about density, as a lemon floats because of the bubbles of air in the skin.

Make a DIY stethoscope with a funnel and a cardboard tube ( you also need some tape).

All you need to make oobleck is cornflour ( cornstarch ) and water. Oobleck is a really unusual substance as it is a liquid normally but a solid when pressure is applied.

Create a colourful pattern with skittles and water. This can be made into an investigation by timing how long it takes the colours to spread out with cold and warm water.

Create a basic density jar with just vegetable oil and water. This is a great first density demonstration as the less dense oil floats on the water.

Learn about string shapes and basic engineering with rolls of newspaper and tape!

Learn about density with small boats made from aluminium foil. This can be made into an investigation by experimenting to discover how many coins it takes to make each boat sink.

Find out how to stand on a paper cup without breaking it with a stack of paper cups and sheets of thick cardboard.

Find out how to pierce a potato with a straw with a simple science trick!

Make a straw flute with just straws and tape!

Demonstrate the Bernoulli principle with an empty plastic bottle and a piece of paper.

Make a rain gauge with a plastic bottle and a ruler.

Create a sundial with a straw or pencil and some play dough!

Do you have any more two ingredient science experiment ideas to share?

#3388. Neuer Gebäudetyp E: Schnelles und günstiges Bauen per Gesetz

Deutschland braucht mehr bezahlbaren Wohnraum. Das soll unter anderem mit dem Gebäudetyp E gelingen - das E steht hier für "einfach". Der Gesetzentwurf liegt dem ARD-Hauptstadtstudio exklusiv vor.

Wer Bundesbauministerin Verena Hubertz von der SPD auf den Gebäudetyp E anspricht, bekommt von ihr gerne die Schlagworte "Bauen ohne Schnickschnack" zu hören. Tatsächlich bringt diese griffige Formel die Grundidee auf den Punkt: Bauen soll einfacher, schneller und günstiger werden, indem Bauherrn und Planer auf nicht zwingende Standards verzichten können - sowohl bei Einfamilien- als auch bei Mehrfamilienhäusern.

Beim Gebäudetyp E geht es darum, zwischen wirklich notwendigen Anforderungen und Ausstattungen zu unterscheiden, die zwar Komfort mit sich bringen, aber das Bauen auch teuer machen. Solche Komfortstandards haben sich in den vergangenen Jahren etabliert, als das Bauen dank niedriger Kreditzinsen vergleichsweise günstig war: etwa eine bestimmte Anzahl von Steckdosen im Wohnzimmer, extra hohe Decken oder besonders dicke Fenster und Wände für den Schallschutz.

Das Bauen ist in Deutschland erneut überdurchschnittlich teurer geworden, weil Wohnraum nach wie vor fehlt. mehr

Vieles davon zählt heute zu den "Anerkannten Regeln der Technik". Weicht ein Architekt oder ein Bauunternehmer davon ab, kann ihm nach aktuellem Vertragsrecht eine Klage des Käufers drohen. Künftig sollen die Beteiligten eines Bauprojekts vorab rechtssicher vereinbaren können, dass sie auf gewisse Standards verzichten. Das ist der Gebäudetyp E - E steht für einfach. Fachleute schätzen, dass die Baukosten bei diesem Konzept um etwa zehn Prozent sinken.

Bundesjustizministerin Stefanie Hubig stellt klar: Es werde keine Abstriche bei der Sicherheit beim Bauen geben, die stehe weiter an oberster Stelle. "Der Gebäudetyp E bietet die Chance für einen echten Befreiungsschlag: Bauen ohne teure Extras. Mit dem Gebäudetyp-E-Gesetz ebnen wir dafür den Weg", sagt die SPD-Politikerin. Und Bauministerin Hubertz ergänzt: "Jeder Euro, den wir nicht in überflüssige Standards stecken, ist ein Euro für mehr bezahlbaren Wohnraum. Deswegen bin ich froh, dass wir jetzt einen Entwurf haben."

Geplant war, dass der Gesetzentwurf schon im Sommer vorliegt. So hatten es beide Ministerinnen im Juli angekündigt. Doch daraus wurde nichts. Innerhalb der schwarz-roten Koalition gab es Unstimmigkeiten in Detailfragen. Unter anderem waren die Koalitionspartner uneins, ob der Gebäudetyp E das "neue Normal" beim Bauen oder eine Spezialkategorie im Vertragsrecht werden soll.

Das Bundesbauministerium will zügig für neuen Wohnraum zu sorgen. Wo bleibt der geplante "Gebäudetyp E"? mehr

Der baupolitische Sprecher der Unionsfraktion, Jan-Marco Luczak, sagte vor wenigen Tagen noch: "Statt teurem Goldstandard müssen wir einfaches und kostengünstiges Bauen zum 'neuen Normal' machen - ohne neuen Vertragstyp." Der Kritikpunkt: Sonst könnte eine Art Zwei-Klassen-Bauen entstehen. Auf der einen Seite ein hoher Baustandard - auf der anderen der Gebäudetyp E mit einem möglicherweise schlechteren Image.

Justizministerin Hubig teilt diese Bedenken nicht. Im Gesetzentwurf, der dem ARD-Hauptstadtstudio vorliegt, ist nun vorgesehen, dass der Gebäudetyp E eine eigene Vertragskategorie wird. Die Rede ist von einer freiwilligen Option für die Parteien eines Bauprojekts. Entscheiden sie sich nicht für den Gebäudetyp E, bleibt es bei den bisherigen Regeln des Vertragsrechts. So sei sichergestellt, dass eine einfachere Bauausführung dem Willen beider Parteien entspreche, heißt es. Anschließende Klagen der Käufer hätten damit keine Chance, Planer und Baufirmen gehen kein Risiko ein.

Die Lage am Immobilienmarkt spitzt sich weiter zu. mehr

Der Entwurf geht jetzt an die Bundesländer und an die Verbände. Sie können Kritik äußern. Änderungen im Detail sind im Gesetzentwurf also noch möglich, bevor die Regierung ihn offiziell auf den Weg bringt. Bis das erste Gebäude auf Basis des neuen Gesetzes steht, dürfte es noch etwas dauern: Nach Planung und Bau wäre das Richtfest realistischerweise in frühestens zwei Jahren denkbar.

Dieses Thema im Programm: tagesschau.de | Gesetzentwurf zum Gebäudetyp E ist fertig | 25.10.2026 | 23:57 Uhr

#3389. Mehrheit in Alberta will weiter zu Kanada gehören

In Kanada haben die Einwohner der wirtschaftsstarken Provinz Alberta über eine mögliche Unabhängigkeit abgestimmt. Die Befürworter fühlen sich von der Regierung in Ottawa benachteiligt. Das Ergebnis fiel deutlich aus.

Die Einwohner der ölreichen Provinz Alberta wollen mehrheitlich Kanadier bleiben. Mehr als 76 Prozent der Wähler stimmten laut vorläufigen Teilergebnissen gegen eine mögliche Abspaltung, wie die Wahlbehörde nach Auszählung von rund 60 Prozent der Wahlkreise bekanntgab.

Bei der Volksabstimmung am Montag ging es darum, ob Alberta ein zweites verbindliches Referendum zur Trennung von Kanada anstreben soll, sowie um neun weitere Fragen zur Einwanderung und zur Verfassung. Die Provinz liegt im Westen des Landes und gilt als besonders konservativ. Viele Unabhängigkeitsbefürworter sind der Ansicht, dass Alberta wirtschaftlich einen großen Beitrag zum Wohlstand Kanadas leistet, von der Regierung in Ottawa aber politisch benachteiligt wird.

Umfragen hatten zuletzt bereits eine große Mehrheit für den Verbleib als Teil Kanadas gesehen - etwa 70 Prozent. Auch die beide großen politischen Parteien des Landes hatten sich immer wieder deutlich gegen eine Abspaltung ausgesprochen. Das endgültige Ergebnis der Abstimmung soll nach Angaben der Wahlbehörde am 29. Oktober veröffentlicht werden.

Aus der US-Regierung von Präsident Donald Trump war im Vorfeld des Referendums Unterstützung für die Unabhängigkeitsbewegung gekommen. Das nun so klare Ergebnis stärkt vor dem Hintergrund von Trumps Annexionsdrohungen und einem Handelskrieg mit Washington das Bestreben von Premierminister Mark Carney, die nationale Einheit zu wahren.

Kanada sei so viel mehr als die Summe seiner Teile, schrieb Carney auf der Online-Plattform X. "Jetzt haben wir die Chance, all das, was uns als Kanadiern gegeben wurde - und uns wurde das großartigste Land der Welt geschenkt - zu nutzen und unser Land für uns alle besser zu machen."

Danielle Smith, die Premierministerin von Alberta, zeigte sich ebenfalls erfreut über die ersten Ergebnisse der Abstimmung. "Die Einwohner Albertas haben für ein stärkeres Alberta in einem vereinten Kanada gestimmt", schrieb sie auf X.

In der Provinzhauptstadt Edmonton herrschte gedrückte Stimmung bei den Befürwortern einer Loslösung von Kanada. Er sei "enttäuscht von seinen Mitbürgern", sagte der Separatist Jour Dain. Er erwäge, in die USA auszuwandern.

Der führende Separatist Keith Wilson sagte laut dem Sender CBC, dass die Sorgen, die sich die Menschen in Alberta hinsichtlich ihres Platzes innerhalb Kanadas machten, nicht mit diesem Referendum enden würden. "Die Debatte über Albertas Zukunft in Kanada - über unseren Platz und unsere Rolle in Kanada - wird weitergehen", so Wilson. Die Separatisten hatten bereits im Voraus angekündigt, auch ein Referendum, das gegen sie ausgeht, nicht als Ende ihrer Bestrebungen zu sehen.

Die Provinz Alberta mit rund fünf Millionen Einwohnern ist das Zentrum der kanadischen Erdöl- und Erdgasindustrie und verfügt über riesige Ölsandvorkommen. Die Klimapolitik der kanadischen Bundesregierung, etwa CO2-Abgaben und Vorgaben für die Energiebranche, wird von Separatisten als Angriff auf die wirtschaftliche Grundlage der Provinz betrachtet.

In Kanada hat es in der Vergangenheit bereits zwei Unabhängigkeits-Referenden gegeben - allerdings beide in der französischsprachigen Provinz Québec. Dort geht es weniger um wirtschaftliche Fragen als um sprachliche und kulturelle Unterschiede. Anfang Oktober ging dort die separatistische Parti Québécois als stärkste Kraft aus der Regionalwahl hervor. Sie strebt ein weiteres Referendum über die Unabhängigkeit der Provinz an. Umfragen zufolge gibt es dafür derzeit allerdings ebenfalls keine Mehrheit in der Bevölkerung.
